Unlicensed drivers have been targeted by the government and taxi drivers in the past, mainly due to the lack of [third-party insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third-party_insurance) for passengers, and because taxi drivers feared competition from drivers of these ride-hailing services.[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299] In 2024, the government looked into legalising these services.[^cite_note-300][Bus services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_services_in_Hong_Kong) operate more than 700 routes across the territory,[^cite_note-LegcoTransport-291] with smaller [public light buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_light_bus) (also known as minibuses) serving areas standard buses do not reach as frequently or directly.[^cite_note-301] Expressways and truck roads, organised with the [Hong Kong Strategic Route and Exit Number System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Strategic_Route_and_Exit_Number_System), connect all major areas of the territory.[^cite_note-302] The [Hong Kong–Zhuhai–Macau Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong%E2%80%93Zhuhai%E2%80%93Macau_Bridge) provides a direct route to the western side of the Pearl River estuary.[^cite_note-HZMBridge-255]

#### Air



[Hong Kong International Airport](248457.md)



[Cathay Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathay_Pacific), Hong Kong's [Flagcarrier](465816.md)&#x20;

[Hong Kong International Airport](248457.md) is the territory's primary airport, replacing [Kai Tak International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kai_Tak_Airport) which ended operations in 1998.[^cite_note-303]

Over 100 airlines operate flights from the airport, including [Cathay Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathay_Pacific), the [flag carrier](465816.md) of Hong Kong. It has been regarded as a 5-star airline by Skytrax and has been in the world's top 10 list of airlines for multiple consecutive years. [Hong Kong Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Airlines), low-cost airline [HK Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HK_Express), low-cost airline [Greater Bay Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Bay_Airlines), and cargo airline [Air Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Hong_Kong) are all airlines based in Hong Kong.[^cite_note-304]

Hong Kong International Airport was the [eighth-busiest airport by passenger traffic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_busiest_airports_by_passenger_traffic)[^cite_note-305] pre-COVID and handles [the most air-cargo traffic in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_busiest_airports_by_cargo_traffic).[^cite_note-306]

Most private recreational aviation in Hong Kong, flies through [Shek Kong Airfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shek_Kong_Airfield), under the supervision of the [Hong Kong Aviation Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Aviation_Club).[^cite_note-307]

#### Sea



Tsim Sha Tsui Ferry Pier

The [Port of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Hong_Kong), located by the [South China Sea](74209.md), is a deepwater [seaport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seaport) dominated by trade in [containerised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Containerisation) manufactured products. A key factor in the economic development of Hong Kong, the natural shelter and deep waters of [Victoria Harbour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_Harbour) provide ideal conditions for berthing and the handling of all types of vessels. It is one of the [busiest ports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World's_busiest_port) in the world, in the three categories of shipping movements, cargo handled, and passengers carried. This makes Hong Kong a Large-Port Metropolis.[^cite_note-tss-308] The [Star Ferry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_Ferry) operates two lines across Victoria Harbour for its 53,000 daily passengers.[^cite_note-309] Ferries also serve outlying islands inaccessible by other means. Smaller [kai-to](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kai-to) boats serve the most remote coastal settlements.[^cite_note-310] Ferry travel to Macau and mainland China is also available.[^cite_note-311] [Junks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junk_\(ship\)), once common in Hong Kong waters, are no longer widely available and are used privately and for tourism.[^cite_note-312] The large size of the port gives Hong Kong the classification of Large-Port Metropolis.[^cite_note-tss-308]

### Utilities

Main articles: [Energy in Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Hong_Kong), [Electricity sector in Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity_sector_in_Hong_Kong), and [Water supply and sanitation in Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Hong_Kong)



[Lamma Power Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamma_Power_Station), a coal power plant on [Lamma Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamma_Island) owned by [Hongkong Electric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hongkong_Electric_Company)

Hong Kong generates most of its electricity locally.[^cite_note-energystats2016p1-313] The vast majority of this energy comes from fossil fuels, with 46% from coal and 47% from petroleum.[^cite_note-314] The rest is from other imports, including nuclear energy generated in mainland China.[^cite_note-315] Renewable sources account for a negligible amount of energy generated for the territory.[^cite_note-316] Small-scale wind-power sources have been developed,[^cite_note-energystats2016p1-313] and a small number of private homes and public buildings have installed solar panels.[^cite_note-317]

With few natural lakes and rivers, high population density, inaccessible groundwater sources, and extremely seasonal rainfall, the territory lacks a reliable freshwater supply. The [Dong River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dong_River_\(China\)) in Guangdong supplies 70% of the city's water,[^cite_note-LeeWater-318] with the remaining demand met by harvesting rainwater locally.[^cite_note-319] Toilets in most built-up areas of the territory flush with seawater, which reduces freshwater use.[^cite_note-LeeWater-318]

[Broadband](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadband) Internet access is widely available, with 99.3% of households connected. Connections over [fibre-optic infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiber_to_the_x) are increasingly prevalent,[^cite_note-320] contributing to the high regional average connection speed of 21.9 Mbit/s (the world's fourth-fastest).[^cite_note-321] Mobile-phone use is ubiquitous;[^cite_note-322] there are almost 22 million [mobile-phone accounts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_mobile_phones_in_use) registered in Hong Kong,[^cite_note-323] which is almost triple the territory's population.

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Hong_Kong)

See also: [Cantonese culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese_culture)

Hong Kong culture is characterised as a hybrid of [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_world) and [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture) culture. Traditional Chinese values emphasising family and education blend with Western ideals, including economic liberty and the rule of law.[^cite_note-324] Although the vast majority of the population is ethnically Chinese, Hong Kong has developed a distinct identity. The territory diverged from the mainland through its long period of colonial administration and a different pace of economic, social, and cultural development. Mainstream culture was derived from immigrants originating from various parts of China; it was then influenced by British-style education, a separate political system, and the territory's rapid development during the late 20th century.[^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326] Most migrants of that era fled poverty and war, reflected in the prevailing attitude toward wealth; Hongkongers would tend to link self-image and decision-making to material benefits.[^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328] Residents' sense of local identity has increased post-handover: polling in December 2022 had 32% of respondents identifying as "Hongkongers", 34.1% identifying as "Hongkongers in China" 45.9% purporting a "Mixed Identity", 20.5% identifying as "Chinese" and 11.9% identifying as "Chinese in Hong Kong".[^cite_note-329]

Traditional Chinese family values, including [family honour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_honor), [filial piety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filial_piety), and a [preference for sons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_selection), are prevalent.[^cite_note-330] [Nuclear families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_family) are the most common households, although multi-generational and extended families are not unusual.[^cite_note-331] Spiritual concepts such as *[feng shui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feng_shui)* are observed; large-scale construction projects often hire consultants to ensure proper building positioning and layout. The degree of its adherence to *feng shui* is believed to determine a business's success.[^cite_note-FengShui-201] *[Bagua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagua)* mirrors are regularly used to deflect evil spirits,[^cite_note-332] and buildings often lack [floor numbers with a 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_numerology);[^cite_note-333] the number has a similar sound to the word for "die" in Cantonese.[^cite_note-334]

### Cuisine

Main article: [Hong Kong cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_cuisine)

An assortment of items in a Dim Sum breakfast meal

French Toast on left, Milk Tea on right

Typical fare at a dim sum restaurant (left); *[cha chaan teng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cha_chaan_teng)* breakfast food with [Hong Kong-style milk tea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong-style_milk_tea) (right)

Food in Hong Kong is primarily based on [Cantonese cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese_cuisine), despite the territory's exposure to foreign influences and its residents' varied origins. Rice is the staple food, and is usually served plain with other dishes.[^cite_note-LongHKFood-335] Freshness of ingredients is emphasised. Poultry and seafood are commonly sold live at [wet markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_market), and ingredients are used as quickly as possible when still fresh.[^cite_note-336] There are up to five daily meals: breakfast, lunch, afternoon tea, dinner, and *[siu yeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siu_yeh)*.[^cite_note-337] [Dim sum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dim_sum), as part of *[yum cha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yum_cha)* (brunch), is a dining-out tradition with family and friends. Dishes include [congee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congee), *[cha siu bao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cha_siu_bao)*, *[siu yuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siu_yuk)*, [egg tarts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egg_tart), and [mango pudding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mango_pudding). Local versions of Western food are served at *[cha chaan teng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cha_chaan_teng)* (Hong Kong-style cafes). Common *cha chaan teng* menu items include macaroni in soup, deep-fried French toast, and [Hong Kong-style milk tea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong-style_milk_tea).[^cite_note-LongHKFood-335]

As of 2025, the [Michelin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelin_guide) Guide listed 76 starred restaurants in total. It awarded 7 restaurants 3 Michelin Stars, 11 restaurants with 2 Michelin Stars, and 58 restaurants with 1 Michelin Star. It also gave 4 Green Stars for sustainability, Amber, Feuille, Mora, and Roganic. Alongside the starred list, 65 restaurants received [Bib Gourmand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelin_Guide) status for good quality food at good value.

### Language

Main article: [Languages of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Hong_Kong)

The predominant language today is [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese), a [variety of Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varieties_of_Chinese) originating in Guangzhou. It is spoken by 93.7% of the population, 88.2% as a first language and 5.5% as a second language.[^cite_note-2021By-CensusLanguages-3] Slightly over half the population (58.7%) speaks [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language), the other official language;[^cite_note-OfficialLanguagesOrd-2] 4.6% are native speakers, and 54.1% speak English as a second language.[^cite_note-2021By-CensusLanguages-3] [Code-switching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Code-switching_in_Hong_Kong), mixing English and Cantonese in informal conversation, is common among the bilingual population.[^cite_note-338] Post-handover governments have promoted [Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandarin_Chinese), which is currently about as prevalent as English; 54.2% of the population speaks Mandarin, with 2.3% native speakers and 51.9% as a second language.[^cite_note-2021By-CensusLanguages-3] [Traditional Chinese characters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_characters) are used in writing, rather than the [simplified characters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simplified_Chinese_characters) used in the [mainland](55079.md).[^cite_note-339]

Before the [First Opium War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Opium_War), Hong Kong had no significant [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese)-speaking population. Instead, most people spoke [Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka_Chinese) varieties. However, large areas with speakers of other [Yue Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yue_Chinese) varieties, namely [Weitou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weitou_dialect) and [Tanka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanka_dialect), can be found in the northern New Territories and southern coastal areas, respectively. [Hong Kong Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Hakka) is a variety of Neo-Hakka, and belongs to the [Mei-Hui Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mei-Hui_Hakka?action=edit\&redlink=1) subbranch of [Yuetai Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuetai_Hakka?action=edit\&redlink=1), making it closely related to that of [Meixian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meixian_dialect).[^cite_note-340] Weitou Yue is a variety that is closely related to the Yue Chinese varieties spoken in [Bao'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bao'an,_Shenzhen) and [Dongguan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dongguan), and was the primary language of the [Five Great Clans of the New Territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Great_Clans_of_the_New_Territories), brought into Hong Kong during the [Song dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_dynasty) from [Jiangxi](173817.md).[^cite_note-distributionNT-341] Some of the native Yue Chinese varieties were noticeably Hakka-influenced.[^cite_note-342] Smaller communities of [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien) speakers also existed, and many villages hosted more than one ethnolinguistic group.[^cite_note-distributionNT-341]

Today, communities of speakers of other Chinese varieties (such as [Teochew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teochew_Min), [Sze Yap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siyi_Yue), [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien), and [Shanghainese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghainese)) also live in Hong Kong. Southeast Asian languages such as [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language) and [Malay-Indonesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language) speaking communities also live in Hong Kong. Other minority languages with significant speaker bases in Hong Kong include [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language), [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language), [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language), [Italian](14708.md), [Spanish](26825.md), [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language), [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic), [Hindustani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindustani_language), [Japanese](15606.md), and [Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_language), among others.[^cite_note-343]

### Cinema

Main article: [Cinema of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Hong_Kong)

Statue of Bruce Lee in a fighting pose

Statue of [Bruce Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Lee) on the [Avenue of Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avenue_of_Stars,_Hong_Kong), a tribute to the city's film industry

Hong Kong developed into a filmmaking hub during the late 1940s as a wave of Shanghai filmmakers migrated to the territory, and these movie veterans helped build the colony's entertainment industry over the next decade.[^cite_note-344] By the 1960s, the city was well known to overseas audiences through films such as *[The World of Suzie Wong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_of_Suzie_Wong_\(film\))*.[^cite_note-345] When [Bruce Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Lee)'s *[The Way of the Dragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Way_of_the_Dragon)* was released in 1972 and *[Enter the Dragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enter_the_Dragon)* in 1973, local productions became popular outside Hong Kong. During the 1980s and 1900s, films such as *[A Better Tomorrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Better_Tomorrow)*, *[As Tears Go By](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/As_Tears_Go_By_\(film\))*, *[God of Gamblers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_of_Gamblers)*, *[Police Story](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_Story_\(1985_film\))* and *[Zu Warriors from the Magic Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zu_Warriors_from_the_Magic_Mountain)* expanded global interest beyond [martial arts films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_arts_film); locally made gangster films, romantic dramas, and supernatural fantasies became popular.[^cite_note-346]



[Jackie Chan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackie_Chan)

Hong Kong cinema continued to be internationally successful over the following decades with critically acclaimed movies and dramas such as *[Farewell My Concubine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farewell_My_Concubine_\(film\))*, *[To Live](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/To_Live_\(1994_film\))*, *[Shaolin Soccer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaolin_Soccer)*, *[Kung Fu Hustle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kung_Fu_Hustle)*, *[Ip Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ip_Man_\(film_series\))*, *[Drunken Master II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drunken_Master_II)*, *[In the Mood for Love](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_the_Mood_for_Love)* and *[Chungking Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chungking_Express)*. The city's martial arts film roots are evident in the roles of the most prolific Hong Kong actors. [Bruce Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Lee), [Jackie Chan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackie_Chan), [Stephen Chow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Chow), [Jet Li](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_Li), [Chow Yun-fat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chow_Yun-fat), [Donnie Yen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donnie_Yen), [Tony Leung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Leung_Chiu-wai), [Karen Mok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karen_Mok), [Maggie Q](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maggie_Q) and [Michelle Yeoh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Yeoh) are some of the most popular and well known actors and actresses. Hong Kong films have also grown popular in overseas markets such as Japan, South Korea, Indonesia, Taiwan and the US, earning the city the moniker "Hollywood of the East".[^cite_note-347] At the height of the local movie industry in the early 1990s, over 400 films were produced each year; since then, industry momentum has shifted to mainland China. The number of films produced annually has declined to about 60 in 2017.[^cite_note-348]

### Music

Main article: [Music of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Hong_Kong)

Leslie Cheung with a microphone

A serious-looking Andy Lau, seated and wearing a suit

[Leslie Cheung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leslie_Cheung) (left) is considered a pioneering Cantopop artist, and [Andy Lau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andy_Lau) has been an icon of Hong Kong music and film for several decades as a member of the [Four Heavenly Kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Heavenly_Kings_\(Hong_Kong\)).

[Cantopop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantopop) is a genre of Cantonese popular music which emerged in Hong Kong during the 1970s. Evolving from Shanghai-style *[shidaiqu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shidaiqu)*, it is also influenced by [Cantonese opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese_opera) and Western pop.[^cite_note-349] Local media featured songs by artists and bands such as [Sam Hui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Hui), [Anita Mui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anita_Mui), [Leslie Cheung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leslie_Cheung), [Alan Tam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Tam), [Hacken Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hacken_Lee) and [Beyond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beyond_\(band\)); during the 1980s, exported films and shows exposed Cantopop to a global audience.[^cite_note-350] The genre's popularity peaked in the 1990s, when the [Four Heavenly Kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Heavenly_Kings_\(Hong_Kong\)) dominated Asian record charts.[^cite_note-351] Despite a general decline since late in the decade,[^cite_note-352] Cantopop remains dominant in Hong Kong; contemporary artists such as [Eason Chan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eason_Chan), [Jackson Wang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson_Wang), [Joey Yung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joey_Yung), [G.E.M.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G.E.M.), [Tyson Yoshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyson_Yoshi), [Twins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twins_\(group\)) and [Mirror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirror_\(group\)) are popular in the territory and around the world.[^cite_note-353]

Western classical music has historically had a strong presence in Hong Kong and remains a large part of local musical education.[^cite_note-354] The publicly funded [Hong Kong Philharmonic Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Philharmonic_Orchestra), the territory's oldest professional symphony orchestra, frequently hosts musicians and conductors from all over the world. The [Hong Kong Chinese Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Chinese_Orchestra), composed of [classical Chinese instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_orchestra), is the leading Chinese ensemble and plays a significant role in promoting traditional music in the city.[^cite_note-355]

Hong Kong has never had a separate national anthem from the country that controlled it; its current official national anthem is therefore that of the People's Republic of China, "[March of the Volunteers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_of_the_Volunteers)". The song "[Glory to Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glory_to_Hong_Kong)" has been used by [protestors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%932020_Hong_Kong_protests) as an unofficial anthem of the city.[^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357]

### Galleries and museums



**[M+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M+)**



[Hong Kong Museum of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Museum_of_Art)&#x20;

Hong Kong is home to a wide range of museums, galleries, and cultural institutions, and is regarded as one of Asia's leading centres for art, heritage, and visual culture. Major cultural development in the city has taken place in the [West Kowloon Cultural District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Kowloon_Cultural_District), a large arts and museum quarter on reclaimed land in Kowloon. Its best-known institutions include [M+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M+), a museum of visual culture focused on 20th and 21st century art, design, architecture, and moving image, and the [Hong Kong Palace Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Palace_Museum), which displays Chinese artworks and artifacts. Apart from West Kowloon, Hong Kong has many additional museums across the territory. The [Hong Kong Museum of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Museum_of_Art) in [Tsim Sha Tsui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsim_Sha_Tsui) houses collections of Chinese antiquities, calligraphy, painting, and Hong Kong art. The [Hong Kong Heritage Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Heritage_Museum) in [Sha Tin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sha_Tin) focuses on history, art, and local popular culture, with exhibitions on Cantonese opera, film, and design. The [Hong Kong Museum of History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Museum_of_History) presents the development of Hong Kong from prehistoric times to the present, while the [Hong Kong Science Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Science_Museum) and [Hong Kong Space Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Space_Museum) are among the city's main public institutions devoted to science and education. [Tai Kwun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tai_Kwun), a former police station and prison compound in [Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central,_Hong_Kong), has been revitalised as a centre for heritage and contemporary art, and hosts exhibitions, performances, and public programmes.



[Tai Kwun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tai_Kwun)

Hong Kong also has a large commercial and independent gallery scene. [Central district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central,_Hong_Kong) has long been the city's main gallery district, with international auction houses and global galleries operating alongside local art spaces. [Wong Chuk Hang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wong_Chuk_Hang) has developed into another important arts area, with former industrial buildings converted into galleries, studios, and exhibition venues. The city hosts major international art events, including [Art Basel Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Basel) and Art Central, which attract galleries, collectors, and artists from around the world.

### Sport

Main article: [Sport in Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Hong_Kong)

Cheering rugby fans, seen from the stands

The [Hong Kong Sevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Sevens), considered the premier tournament of the [World Rugby Sevens Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Rugby_Sevens_Series)

The city has hosted numerous major sporting events, including the inaugural [AFC Asian Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AFC_Asian_Cup), the [2008 Summer Olympics equestrian events](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equestrian_at_the_2008_Summer_Olympics), the [2009 East Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_East_Asian_Games), the [Rugby World Cup Sevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_World_Cup_Sevens) in 1997 and 2005, the 2005 [World Squash Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Squash_Championships) and [2025 World Grand Prix (snooker)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_World_Grand_Prix_\(snooker\)).[^cite_note-ShenKee-358]

The territory regularly hosts the [Hong Kong Sevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Sevens), [Hong Kong Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Marathon), [Hong Kong Open (tennis)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Open_\(tennis\)), [Hong Kong Tennis Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Tennis_Classic) and [LIV Golf Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LIV_Golf_Hong_Kong). Hong Kong represents itself separately from mainland China, with its own sports teams in international competitions.[^cite_note-ShenKee-358] The territory has participated in almost every Summer Olympics since 1952 (with the exception of its support of the [1980 Summer Olympics boycott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Summer_Olympics_boycott)) and has earned [nine medals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_at_the_Olympics). [Lee Lai-shan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Lai-shan) won the territory's first Olympic gold medal at the [1996 Atlanta Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Summer_Olympics),[^cite_note-359] and [Cheung Ka Long](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheung_Ka_Long) won the second one in [Tokyo 2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Summer_Olympics).[^cite_note-360] Hong Kong athletes have won [126 medals at the Paralympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_at_the_Paralympics) and [17 at the Commonwealth Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_at_the_Commonwealth_Games). Although they are no longer part of the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations), the city's last appearance was in [1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Commonwealth_Games).[^cite_note-361]



[Happy Valley Racecourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Happy_Valley_Racecourse) and [Hong Kong Jockey Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Jockey_Club)

Sporting leagues in Hong Kong include [Hong Kong Premier League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Premier_League), [Hong Kong Premiership (rugby union)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Premiership_\(rugby_union\)), [Hong Kong A1 Division Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_A1_Division_Championship), and the [Hong Kong Ice Hockey League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Ice_Hockey_League). Famous local sporting clubs include [Hong Kong Football Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Football_Club), [South China Athletic Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_China_Athletic_Association), [Kitchee SC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kitchee_SC), and [Hong Kong Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Eastern).

Hong Kong sports have been described as "Club Life". Some clubs have documented their history thoroughly, while others have disappeared along with their past.

- [Hong Kong Cricket Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Cricket_Club)

- Craigengower Cricket Club

- Kowloon Cricket Club

- Kowloon Bowling Green Club

- Indian Recreation Club

- Chinese Recreation Club

- Filipino Club

- United Services Recreation Club

- Club de Recreio

- Club Lusitano

- [Royal Hong Kong Yacht Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Hong_Kong_Yacht_Club)

- Aberdeen Boat Club

[Dragon boat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragon_boat) races originated as a religious ceremony conducted during the annual [Tuen Ng Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragon_Boat_Festival). The race was revived as a modern sport as part of the [Tourism Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Tourism_Board)'s efforts to promote Hong Kong's image abroad. The first modern competition was organised in 1976, and overseas teams began competing in the first international race in 1993.[^cite_note-362]

The [Hong Kong Jockey Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Jockey_Club), the territory's largest taxpayer,[^cite_note-JockeyClub-363] has a monopoly on gambling and provides over 7% of government revenue.[^cite_note-364] Three forms of gambling are legal in Hong Kong: lotteries, horse racing, and football.[^cite_note-JockeyClub-363]

### Entertainment and leisure



[Lan Kwai Fong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lan_Kwai_Fong)



[Hong Kong Disneyland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Disneyland)&#x20;

Hong Kong has two theme parks. [Hong Kong Disneyland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Disneyland) on Lantau Island is a major theme park and resort destination. [Ocean Park Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ocean_Park_Hong_Kong) combines rides and animal exhibits, with zones across the Waterfront and Summit areas.

For big events, [Kai Tak Sports Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kai_Tak_Sports_Park) anchors the city's newest entertainment hub, built on the former airport site, with a 50,000-seat main stadium and a retractable roof. [Hong Kong Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Stadium) remains a key venue for football and major fixtures in Causeway Bay, with a capacity of 40,116. [AsiaWorld-Expo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AsiaWorld-Expo) near the airport hosts concerts, exhibitions, and large-scale conventions across multiple halls and an arena-style space. [Hong Kong Convention and Exhibition Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Convention_and_Exhibition_Centre) is the core waterfront venue in Wan Chai for exhibitions, meetings, and shows.

## Spectator sports

### Badminton



Tiu Keng Leng Sports Centre Badminton courts

Badminton stands as one of the most popular and historically significant sports in Hong Kong,[^cite_note-Sport_in_Hong_Kong-365] governed by the Badminton Association of Hong Kong, China since its establishment in 1934. The sport is designated as a Tier A elite discipline at the [Hong Kong Sports Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Sports_Institute), which provides world-class training and scientific support to the national squad. This institutional backing has allowed the city to consistently produce top-tier talent capable of competing at the [BWF World Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BWF_World_Tour) and the [Olympic Games](22576.md).

The current professional landscape is highlighted by the [mixed doubles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_doubles) pairing of [Tang Chun-man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tang_Chun_Man) and [Tse Ying-suet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tse_Ying_Suet), who achieved a career-high world ranking of number two. The duo remains a dominant force on the international circuit, recently securing a historic gold medal at the 2025 [Badminton Asia Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badminton_Asia_Championships) and a podium finish at the 2026 [All England Open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_England_Open_Badminton_Championships). In the men's singles discipline, [Ng Ka-long Angus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng_Ka_Long) has been a mainstay of the global top ten, reaching a peak ranking of number six. He is supported by teammate [Lee Cheuk-yiu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Cheuk_Yiu). This former [Hong Kong Open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Open_\(badminton\)) champion has maintained a strong international presence with a career-high ranking of thirteen. On the local front, the sport enjoys immense participation through a robust network of community clubs and school programs. The annual Hong Kong Open, a [BWF World Tour Super 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BWF_World_Tour) event is held at the [Hong Kong Coliseum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Coliseum).

### Basketball

See also: [Hong Kong men's national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_men's_national_basketball_team)

Basketball is one of the most popular sports in Hong Kong,[^cite_note-Sport_in_Hong_Kong-365] with the [A1 Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_A1_Division_Championship) Championship serving as the city's top-tier amateur league since 1954. Historically, the sport gained regional prominence through the success of clubs like Regal, which won the 1997 ABC Champions Cup, and more recently, [Hong Kong Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Eastern).

### Cricket

Main article: [Cricket in Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cricket_in_Hong_Kong)

Cricket in Hong Kong has been played since at least 1841. Like most cricketing nations, it was part of the British Empire. The national cricket team has been active since 1866, and the [Hong Kong Cricket Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Cricket_Association) was granted associate membership of the [International Cricket Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Cricket_Council) (ICC) in 1969. Hong Kong hosted the [Hong Kong Cricket Sixes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Cricket_Sixes), an ICC-sanctioned event that features teams of six players in a six-over competition annually till 2012. The [Hong Kong Cricket Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Cricket_Team) qualified for [2014 ICC World Twenty20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_ICC_World_Twenty20) and caused a major upset by defeating hosts Bangladesh.

### Fencing

&#x20;The sport reached its peak during the 2020 and 2024 [Olympic Games](22576.md). [Cheung Ka-long](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheung_Ka-long) became a national icon by winning back-to-back gold medals in men's individual foil, while [Vivian Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vivian_Kong) secured gold in women's individual [épée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89p%C3%A9e) in [Paris 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Summer_Olympics).[^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367]&#x20;



[Hong Kong National Football Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_national_football_team)

### Football

Main article: [Football in Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_Hong_Kong)

Football is one of the most popular sports in Hong Kong, with the [Hong Kong Premier League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Premier_League) being the only fully professional sports league in the city.[^cite_note-Sport_in_Hong_Kong-365][^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370]

### Horse racing



[Longines Hong Kong International Races](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_International_Races)

Horse racing is by far the most popular spectator sport in Hong Kong and generates the largest [horse race gambling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betting_on_horse_racing) turnover in the world. [British tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horseracing_in_the_United_Kingdom) left its mark as one of the most important entertainment and gambling institutions in Hong Kong. Established as the Royal [Hong Kong Jockey Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Jockey_Club) in 1884, the non-profit organization conducts nearly 700 races every season at the two race tracks in [Happy Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Happy_Valley_Racecourse) and [Sha Tin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sha_Tin_Racecourse). The sport annually draws in over 11% of Hong Kong's tax revenue. Off-track betting is available from overseas bookmakers. In 2009, betting on horse races generated an average US\$12.7 million in gambling turnover per race, 6 times larger than its closest rival [France](5843419.md) at US\$2 million, while the [United States](3434750.md) only generated \$250,000. Some consider betting on horse races an investment.

### Rugby union

Main article: [Rugby union in Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_union_in_Hong_Kong)

The [Hong Kong Rugby Football Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Rugby_Football_Union) was founded in 1953. Rugby is long established, partly as a result of its being a [British colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Hong_Kong).

[Hong Kong Sevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Sevens) is held annually in Hong Kong and is considered the premier tournament on the [World Rugby Sevens Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Rugby_Sevens_Series) competition.

[Hong Kong national rugby union team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_national_rugby_union_team) is ranked 23rd in the world with a peak rating of 21st (2018–2022). Hong Kong is one of the best teams in Asia and has finished first place in the [Asia Rugby Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Rugby_Championship) in 2018, 2019, 2022, 2023, 2024, and 2025. They qualified for the [2027 Rugby World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2027_Men's_Rugby_World_Cup) for the first time by winning the [2025 Asia Rugby Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Asia_Rugby_Championship).

The [Hong Kong national rugby sevens team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_national_rugby_sevens_team) is widely regarded as the best [rugby sevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_sevens) team in Asia and a regular participant in the [Rugby World Cup Sevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_World_Cup_Sevens). Hong Kong has won the [Asian Sevens Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Sevens_Series) a record 8 times and the [Asian Games](39205.md) 2 times.

## Education

Main article: [Education in Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Hong_Kong)



Former campus of [St. Paul's College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Paul's_College,_Hong_Kong), the first school established in the colonial era

Education in Hong Kong is managed primarily by the [Hong Kong Education Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Education_Bureau). It is largely modelled on [that of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_the_United_Kingdom), particularly the [English system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_England).[^cite_note-371] Children are required to attend school from age 6 until the completion of secondary education, generally at age 18.[^cite_note-nss-372][^cite_note-HKEduLi-373] At the end of secondary schooling, local school students take a public examination and are awarded the [Hong Kong Diploma of Secondary Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Diploma_of_Secondary_Education) upon successful completion of the exam.[^cite_note-374]

Of residents aged 15 and older, 81% completed lower-secondary education, 66% graduated from an upper secondary school, 32% attended a non-degree tertiary program, and 24% earned a bachelor's degree or higher.[^cite_note-375]

Mandatory education within the city has contributed to an adult [literacy rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy) of around 99.9%.

Comprehensive schools fall into three categories: government-run public schools; subsidised schools, including government-aided and grant schools; and private schools, often run by religious organisations, that base admissions on academic merit. These schools are subject to the Education Bureau's curriculum guidelines. Private schools subsidised under the [Direct Subsidy Scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_Subsidy_Scheme), and international schools fall outside of this system and may elect to use differing curricula and teach using other languages.[^cite_note-HKEduLi-373]

Hong Kong students perform exceptionally well in global benchmarks. In [PISA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment) 2022 (latest available results), Hong Kong ranked in the top tier worldwide; 4th in [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematics) and top 10 in [reading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reading) and [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science). This places it well above the [OECD](33853117.md) average and among the highest-performing systems globally.[^cite_note-376]



[St. Paul's Co-educational College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Paul's_Co-educational_College)

Secondary schools in Hong Kong offer a wide range of curricula. Apart from the [Hong Kong Diploma of Secondary Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Diploma_of_Secondary_Education), international curricula such as the [International Baccalaureate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Baccalaureate), [GCE A-level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GCE_A-level), [IGCSEs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IGCSEs), and [Advanced Placement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advanced_Placement) are prevalent.

Hong Kong secondary schools offer world-class education with top-tier, high-ranking schools such as [St Paul's Co-educational College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Paul's_Co-educational_College), ranked 2nd in Global Top IB Schools 2025 with a 42.4 average score. While schools such as [Diocesan Boys' School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocesan_Boys'_School) and [German Swiss International School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Swiss_International_School) respectively also rank within the top 10.[^cite_note-377]



[Hong Kong International School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_International_School)



[Harrow International School Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrow_International_School_Hong_Kong)

There are also many overseas British independent schools in Hong Kong, such as [Harrow International School Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrow_International_School_Hong_Kong), where 94% of students received grade (A\*-B), Wycombe Abbey School Hong Kong, [Malvern College Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malvern_College_Hong_Kong), and [Shrewsbury International School Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrewsbury_International_School_Hong_Kong).

Moreover, many schools cover various regions of the world, such as [Australian International School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_International_School_Hong_Kong), [American International School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_International_School_Hong_Kong), [Canadian International School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_International_School_of_Hong_Kong), [French International School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_International_School_of_Hong_Kong), and [Korean International School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_International_School_of_Hong_Kong) among others.

Local schools in Hong Kong are categorized into three "bands" based on academic performance, with Band 1 schools being the most prestigious and competitive. These institutions are often criticized for being high-pressure environments. Band 1 schools include [Diocesan Boys' School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocesan_Boys'_School), [Diocesan Girls' School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocesan_Girls'_School), [Ying Wa College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ying_Wa_College), [St. Paul's College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Paul's_College,_Hong_Kong) and [La Salle College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Salle_College).

### Medium of instruction

At primary and secondary school levels, the government maintains a policy of "mother tongue instruction"; most schools use Cantonese as the [medium of instruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medium_of_instruction), with written education in both Chinese and English. Other languages being used as the medium of instruction in non-international school education include [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) and [Putonghua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Chinese) ([Mandarin Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandarin_Chinese)). Secondary schools emphasise "[bi-literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biliteracy) and [tri-lingualism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trilingualism)", which has encouraged the proliferation of spoken Mandarin language education.[^cite_note-378]

English is the official medium of instruction and assessment for most university programmes in Hong Kong, although Cantonese is predominant in informal discussions among local students and professors.[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380][^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382][^cite_note-383][^cite_note-384]

### Tertiary education

Main article: [Higher education in Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_education_in_Hong_Kong)



[University of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Hong_Kong) main building

Hong Kong has twelve universities. The [University of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Hong_Kong) (HKU), ranked [QS Asia #1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QS_World_University_Rankings), was founded as the city's first institute of higher education in 1911 during the early colonial period.[^cite_note-385] The [Chinese University of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_University_of_Hong_Kong) (CUHK) was established in 1963 to fill the need for a university that taught using Chinese as its primary language of instruction.[^cite_note-386] Along with the [Hong Kong University of Science and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_University_of_Science_and_Technology) (HKUST) established in 1991, these universities are consistently ranked among the top 20 or top 50 universities worldwide.[^cite_note-\:1-387][^cite_note-\:2-388][^cite_note-\:3-389]

The [Hong Kong Polytechnic University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Polytechnic_University) (PolyU)[^cite_note-390] and [City University of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_University_of_Hong_Kong) (CityU), both granted university status in 1994, are consistently ranked among the top 50 or top 100 universities worldwide.[^cite_note-\:1-387][^cite_note-\:2-388][^cite_note-\:3-389] The [Hong Kong Baptist University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Baptist_University) (HKBU) was granted university status in 1994[^cite_note-391] and is a liberal arts institution. [Hong Kong Metropolitan University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Metropolitan_University) (formerly as Open University of Hong Kong before 2021) (attaining status in 1997),[^cite_note-392] [Lingnan University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingnan_University) (in 1999),[^cite_note-393] [Hong Kong Shue Yan University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Shue_Yan_University) (in 2006),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELegCo_Grant_to_Shue_Yan_University2007-394] [Education University of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_University_of_Hong_Kong) (in 2016), [Hang Seng University of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hang_Seng_University_of_Hong_Kong) (in 2018) and [Saint Francis University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Francis_University_\(Hong_Kong\)) (in 2024)[^cite_note-395] all attained full university status in subsequent years.



[University of Chicago Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Chicago_Hong_Kong)&#x20;

Overseas universities in Hong Kong often operate as branch campuses or partnerships offering accredited degrees, such as the [University of Chicago Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Chicago_Hong_Kong), [University of Sunderland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Sunderland), [University of Wollongong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Wollongong) in Hong Kong. These institutions provide local and internationally recognized qualifications.

In 2026, [QS Best Student Cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QS_World_University_Rankings) ranked Hong Kong as the 17th best city for university students. 7th in the best Asia for best student cities. Noting high scores in employer activity, desirability, and diverse student population.

**Subject Rankings**



[Hong Kong Academy for Performing Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Academy_for_Performing_Arts)

In the 2026 [QS World University Rankings by Subject](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QS_World_University_Rankings), Hong Kong's higher education sector demonstrated significant global competitiveness, with five academic programs placing within the world's top 10. The [University of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Hong_Kong) (HKU) maintained its status as the city's most decorated institution, securing global 2nd and 5th positions for Dentistry and Education, respectively. Other top-tier performances included the [Chinese University of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CUHK_Faculty_of_Medicine) (CUHK) ranking 6th globally for Nursing, the [Education University of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_University_of_Hong_Kong) (EdUHK) placing 7th in Education, and the [Hong Kong Academy for Performing Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Academy_for_Performing_Arts) (HKAPA) breaking into the top 10 for Performing Arts—the highest-ranked performing arts institution in Asia.

## Healthcare

Main article: [Health in Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Hong_Kong)



[Hong Kong Sanatorium and Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Sanatorium_&_Hospital) ([7th Tallest Hospital in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_hospitals))

Hong Kong is one of the healthiest places in the world, due to early health education, professional health services, and a well-developed healthcare and medication system.



[Queen Mary Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Mary_Hospital_\(Hong_Kong\))&#x20;

Public healthcare in Hong Kong is recognized for its high-quality medical outcomes. The territory has the [highest life expectancy in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_life_expectancy) at 85.9 years. Hong Kong has one of the [lowest infant mortality rates in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_infant_mortality_rate), with recent estimates placing it around 1.7 to 2.5 deaths per 1,000 live births. However, the public system also faces challenges, including high patient volumes, overcrowding in emergency departments, and long wait times for specialist appointments and elective procedures.

Healthcare in Hong Kong is provided through two categories, consisting of [public](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_hospital) and [private hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_hospital). While private hospital services cater to patients seeking shorter wait times and personalized care, the majority of the population relies on the extensive public healthcare system. Public hospitals and clinics are heavily subsidized by the government, ensuring affordable access to a wide range of medical services.

Hong Kong's healthcare system is managed primarily by the [Hospital Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospital_Authority), which was established in 1990 to oversee and operate all public hospitals in the region. The system is organized into seven geographic clusters that group hospitals and associated healthcare facilities based on their distribution across the territory. As of 2026, Hong Kong has a total of 42 [public hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_hospitals) and 11 [private hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_hospitals).

The [University of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Hong_Kong) (HKU) [Li Ka Shing Faculty of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Ka_Shing_Faculty_of_Medicine) is consistently ranked among the top [medical schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_school) globally and is a leader in Asia, ranking 18th worldwide in the [Times Higher Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Times_Higher_Education_World_University_Rankings) 2026 Rankings for [Clinical and Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care) and 24th in the [QS World University Rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QS_World_University_Rankings) [Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicine).

## Media

Main article: [Media of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Hong_Kong)



Hong Kong Newspapers

Modern, green-and-white building with dish antennas on top

[TVB City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TVB_City), headquarters of Hong Kong's first over-the-air television station

Most newspapers in Hong Kong are written in Chinese. There are also English-language newspapers, the major one being the *[South China Morning Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_China_Morning_Post)*, with *[The Standard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Standard_\(Hong_Kong\))* serving as a business-oriented alternative. A variety of Chinese-language newspapers are published daily; the most prominent are *[Ming Pao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ming_Pao)* and *[Oriental Daily News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Daily_News)*. Local publications are often partisan, with pro-Beijing or pro-democracy sympathies. The central government has a print-media presence in the territory through the state-owned *[Ta Kung Pao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta_Kung_Pao)* and *[Wen Wei Po](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wen_Wei_Po)*.[^cite_note-396] Several international publications have regional operations in Hong Kong, including *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)*, the *[Financial Times](136566.md)*, *[USA Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USA_Today)*, *[TimeOut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_Out_\(magazine\))*, *[The Nikkei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Nikkei) and [Vogue Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vogue_Hong_Kong).*[^cite_note-397]

Four [free-to-air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free-to-air) television broadcasters operate in the territory; [TVB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TVB), [HKTVE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HK_Television_Entertainment), [HOY](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I-CABLE_HOY_Limited) and [Phoenix Television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_Television) air eight [digital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_television) channels.[^cite_note-broadcasterList-398][^cite_note-399] TVB, Hong Kong's dominant television network, has an 80% viewer share.[^cite_note-400] [Paid television services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_Hong_Kong) operated by [PCCW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PCCW) offer hundreds of additional channels and cater to a variety of audiences.[^cite_note-401] [RTHK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RTHK) is the public broadcaster, providing seven radio channels and six free-to-air television channels.[^cite_note-402][^cite_note-403][^cite_note-404] Ten non-domestic broadcasters air programming for the territory's foreign population.[^cite_note-broadcasterList-398] Access to media and information over the Internet is not subject to mainland Chinese regulations, including the [Great Firewall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Firewall), but [local internet censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_censorship_in_Hong_Kong) applies.[^cite_note-405]

## In popular culture

Hong Kong finds mention in various popular culture movies, series, and books.[^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407]
